Torus Knots and Minimal Models Revisited : Rational VOA characters from non-hyperbolic knots Preprint:
Dongmin Gang, Byoungyoon Park, Huijoon Sohn

Abstract
In 2003, Hikami and Kirillov uncovered an intriguing connection between torus knots 𝒦 ( P , Q ) \mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)} and Virasoro minimal models ℳ ⁡ ( P , Q ) \mathcal{M}(P,Q) by relating the Kashaev invariants of the knots to the characters of the corresponding minimal models. In this work, we recover and extend this connection by combining the 3D–3D correspondence with a bulk–boundary correspondence. More concretely, we study the 3D 𝒩 = 2 \mathcal{N}=2 gauge theories associated with torus-knot complements via the Dimofte–Gaiotto–Gukov construction and show that, in the infrared, these theories either flow to a unitary TQFT (when | P − Q | = 1 |P-Q|=1 ), whose boundary chiral algebra reproduces that of the associated unitary minimal model, or to a 3D 𝒩 = 4 \mathcal{N}=4 rank-0 SCFT (when | P − Q | > 1 |P-Q|>1 ), which realizes the corresponding non-unitary chiral minimal model at the boundary after an appropriate topological twist. This framework yields new Nahm-sum–like expressions for the characters of Virasoro minimal models and other related rational conformal field theories, providing a systematic algorithm for constructing characters of rational VOAs directly from the combinatorial data of an ideal triangulation of a non-hyperbolic knot complement.



1
Introduction and Summary

A hallmark of research in string/M-theory and quantum field theory is the
discovery of unexpected—and often profound—connections between seemingly
distant areas of physics and mathematics. In some cases, such connections first arise from physical reasoning and
are only later recognized as precise mathematical statements.
In others, relations uncovered within mathematics find their natural
home in a physical framework, where an underlying physical picture not
only clarifies their origin but also points toward systematic
generalizations beyond the original setting.

The present work belongs to the latter category. In 2003, Hikami and Kirillov uncovered a remarkable relation between torus
knots
\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}
and Virasoro minimal models

\mathcal{M}(P,Q)
by observing that the Kashaev invariants of torus knots
are intimately related to the characters of the corresponding minimal
models
Hikami:2003tb
, thereby building on earlier insights of
Zagier and Lawrence
zagier1999modular
.
This striking observation strongly hints at an underlying physical structure that has yet to be fully elucidated. In this paper, we aim to understand and systematically extend the Hikami–Kirillov relation from the perspective of quantum field theory and M-theory. Our approach is based on the
3D–3D correspondence
, which associates
three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric gauge theories to knot
complements, together with a
bulk–boundary correspondence
that relates bulk
three-dimensional topological quantum field theories to rational vertex
operator algebras (r-VOA) living at two-dimensional boundaries. A schematic summary of our proposal is shown in
1
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\;\text{3D--3D }\;\;}\;T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]\simeq\begin{cases}\text{unitary TQFT},&|P-Q|=1,\\[3.00003pt]
\text{3D }\mathcal{N}=4\text{ rank-}0\text{ SCFT},&|P-Q|>1,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\;\;\;\text{bulk--boundary }\;\;\;\;}\;\begin{cases}\text{unitary VOA of }\mathcal{M}(P,Q)\textrm{ or other unitary r-VOAs},\\[3.00003pt]
\text{non-unitary VOA of }\mathcal{M}(P,Q)\textrm{ or other non-unitary r-VOAs}.\end{cases}

Figure 1
:

Schematic summary of the main proposal of this work.
The 3D
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric gauge theory

T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]
, associated with a torus-knot
complement via the 3D–3D correspondence, exhibits two distinct infrared
behaviors.
For
|P-Q|=1
, the theory flows to a unitary TQFT, whereas for
|P-Q|>1

it flows to a three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=4
rank-
0
superconformal
field theory.
The subsequent bulk–boundary correspondence then realizes, depending on
the choice of boundary conditions, the rational vertex operator algebra
of the corresponding Virasoro minimal model
\mathcal{M}(P,Q)
or other
closely related rational conformal field theories
at the boundary.

The 3D–3D correspondence
Terashima:2011qi
;
Dimofte:2011ju
has its origin in the six-dimensional
A_{1}

(2,0)

theory, which describes the low-energy effective world-volume dynamics of two
coincident M5-branes in M-theory.
A twisted compactification of this theory on a three-manifold
M
yields a
three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
theory
T[M]
; see
(
1
) for a detailed discussion in the case where

M=S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}
is a knot complement.
While this six-dimensional picture provides the conceptual foundation,
a concrete and computable description of
T[M]
is furnished by the
bottom-up construction of Dimofte–Gaiotto–Gukov (DGG)
Dimofte:2011ju
,
in which
T[M]
is described as the infrared fixed point of a
supersymmetric abelian Chern–Simons–matter theory.
The validity of this bottom-up approach is supported by the 3D–3D relation,
which equates supersymmetric partition functions of
T[M]
with those of

SL(2,\mathbb{C})
Chern–Simons theory on
M
; this relation
provided the primary motivation for proposing the bottom-up construction,
together with parallel developments in supersymmetric localization
for three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
gauge theories

Kim:2009wb
;
Kapustin:2009kz
;
Imamura:2011su
;
Jafferis:2010un
;
Hama:2010av
;
Hama:2011ea

and state-integral models for complex Chern–Simons theory

HIKAMI_2001
;
Dimofte:2009yn
;
Dimofte:2011gm
;
EllegaardAndersen:2011vps
;
Dimofte:2012qj
;
Dimofte:2014zga
.

The bulk–boundary correspondence between three-dimensional topological field theories
and two-dimensional rational conformal field theories can be traced back to
Witten’s seminal work on Chern–Simons theory
zbMATH04092352
.
In this unitary setting, the correspondence between bulk topological
data and boundary rational vertex operator algebras is by now well
established.
More recently, however, it has been realized that this paradigm can be
substantially generalized beyond unitary TQFTs.
In particular, three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=4
rank-
0

superconformal field theories
Gang:2018huc
;
Gang:2021hrd
provide a natural framework in which
a non-unitary version of the bulk–boundary correspondence emerges,
realizing non-unitary rational vertex operator algebras at the boundary after topological twistings
Gang:2023rei
;
Gang:2022kpe
;
Ferrari:2023fez
.
1
1

1

Upon relaxing the rationality assumption, one may consider a more general
bulk–boundary correspondence
Costello:2018fnz
;
Costello:2020ndc
associates boundary vertex operator
algebras to a wider class of supersymmetric three-dimensional gauge theories.

Rank-
0
theories, characterized by trivial Coulomb and Higgs branches,
are a recently discovered class of exotic yet simple

\mathcal{N}=4
superconformal field theories and have attracted growing
attention from various perspectives
dedushenko:2018bpp
;
Gang:2023ggt
;
Dedushenko:2023cvd
;
Creutzig:2024ljv
;
ArabiArdehali:2024ysy
;
Gaiotto:2024ioj
;
ArabiArdehali:2024vli
;
Kim:2024dxu
;
Gang:2024loa
;
Go:2025ixu
;
Jeong:2025xid
;
Kim:2025klh
;
Kim:2025rog
;
Nishinaka:2025ytu
.

From a direct field-theoretic analysis, we find that the three-dimensional

\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric gauge theories associated with torus-knot
complements
S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}
, constructed via the explicit
algorithm of DGG
Dimofte:2011ju
, exhibit two qualitatively distinct
infrared behaviors depending on the values of
(P,Q)
.
When
|P-Q|=1
, the theory develops a mass gap and flows in the infrared to
a unitary topological quantum field theory.
In contrast, when
|P-Q|>1
, the theory undergoes a non-trivial enhancement of
supersymmetry and flows to a three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=4
rank-
0

superconformal field theory.
Although this infrared fixed point is unitary, it admits topologically twisted
sectors that play a central role in our analysis. These two distinct behaviors mirror precisely the properties of the associated Virasoro
minimal models.
For
|P-Q|=1
, the corresponding minimal model is unitary, and it is therefore
natural that the bulk theory is gapped and topological in the infrared.
For
|P-Q|>1
, on the other hand, the associated minimal model is non-unitary,
and the bulk theory is instead realized as a rank-
0
superconformal field
theory, from which the relevant non-unitary topological data emerge only after
an appropriate topological twist. Accordingly, we expect that for
|P-Q|=1
the bulk theory supports, upon
imposing suitable supersymmetric boundary conditions, the vertex operator
algebra of the corresponding unitary minimal model at the boundary.
For
|P-Q|>1
, the bulk theory is instead expected to support the vertex
operator algebra of the corresponding non-unitary minimal model only after an
appropriate topological twist of the bulk theory, together with a suitable
choice of boundary condition.

To probe the boundary rational vertex operator algebras, we make use of the
half-index
Gadde:2013wq
;
Yoshida:2014ssa
;
Dimofte:2017tpi
, defined as the supersymmetric partition function on

D_{2}\times S^{1}
.
For appropriate supersymmetric boundary conditions, the half-index is expected
to compute the vacuum character of the boundary rational VOA.
In principle, one could fix a given bulk UV gauge-theory description and
systematically scan over all admissible supersymmetric boundary conditions in
order to identify those that realize a rational boundary VOA.
Instead, we adopt the opposite strategy.
We fix the boundary condition to the simplest Dirichlet boundary condition and
instead vary the duality frame of the bulk theory.
In a suitable duality frame, this Dirichlet boundary condition becomes precisely
the boundary condition that supports a rational VOA, and the corresponding
half-index reproduces its vacuum character.
One of the key advantages of the Dimofte–Gaiotto–Gukov (DGG) construction is that
it provides an explicit and systematic prescription for how the gauge-theory
description transforms under changes of duality frame, see (
45
), rendering this approach
both practical and computationally efficient.
This strategy allows us not only to recover the characters of Virasoro minimal
models, but also to compute the characters of other closely related rational
conformal field theories.

Summary for Mathematicians

Motivated by the physical considerations described above, we propose a
systematic procedure for constructing candidates for characters of rational
vertex operator algebras (r-VOAs), realized as half-indices, starting from the
combinatorial data of an ideal triangulation of a torus-knot complement.
See the conjecture in (
195
).

\displaystyle S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\;\text{ideal triangulation}\;\;}\;\text{extended NZ data }(A,B,\bm{\nu}_{x};\mathbf{Q})\ \text{and}\ (A_{\rm r},B_{\rm r},\bm{\nu}_{x,{\rm r}};\mathbf{Q}_{\rm r})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\;\text{half-index}\;\;}\text{ candidates for characters of r-VOAs}

Figure 2
:
Schematic summary of the construction of r-VOA characters from the
combinatorial data of an ideal triangulation of

S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}
.

The construction proceeds as follows.
First, we define
refined
Neumann–Zagier data
(A,B,\bm{\nu}_{x})

associated with a given ideal triangulation.
Here “refined” means that the internal edges are decomposed into “easy” and
“hard” edges, as defined in (
23
), and that the
corresponding Neumann–Zagier matrices
(A,B)
are constructed using the
relations in (
25
).
See also (
72
) for the reduced Neumann–Zagier data

(A_{\rm r},B_{\rm r},\bm{\nu}_{x,{\rm r}})
. Next, we introduce an additional integer-valued
r\times r
matrix

\mathbf{Q}
, subject to the conditions listed in (
32
).
Given these data, we define a family of half-indices in
(
208
),
(
210
), and
(
215
). We conjecture that, up to an overall prefactor of the form
q^{\mathbb{Q}}
,
these half-indices provide candidates for characters of rational VOAs
whenever the additional conditions
(
209
),
(
211
), and
(
218
)
are satisfied.
This conjecture is supported by a large number of explicit examples in Appendix
A
.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we review the DGG construction, with
several clarifications and refinements, focusing in particular on the
role of splitting internal edges into
easy
and
hard
ones
and on the associated flavor symmetries. We also introduce a convenient
modification of the construction that allows us to identify a UV
gauge-theory description suitable for efficient computations of the
half-index.
In particular, for torus-knot complements, we propose a simpler
gauge-theory description,

T^{\rm(r)}[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]
.
In Section
3
, we propose the infrared
phases of
T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]
and explain how these
phases can be verified through computations of BPS partition functions.
Section
4
constitutes the main part of
the paper, where we present explicit formulas for the half-indices of

T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]
, which are completely determined
by the combinatorial data of ideal triangulations, together with the
main conjecture of the paper stated in (
195
).
Finally, in the appendices, we provide numerous examples supporting the
conjecture, along with reviews of the state-integral models for

SL(2,\mathbb{C})
Chern–Simons theories and character formulas for
simple rational VOAs relevant to the examples.

2
T[M]
for torus knot complement
M=S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}

In this section, we review the field theoretic construction of 3D
\mathcal{N}=2
gauge theory
T[M]
labeled by 3-manifold
M
proposed by Dimofte-Gaiotto-Gukov (DGG)
Dimofte:2011ju
. In the paper, we consider the case when the 3-manifold is torus knot complement
M=S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}
and will review the construction for general knot complement on
S^{3}
,
M=S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}
.

T[M]
from a twisted compactification of 6D theory

The 3D theory can be regarded as an effective field theory obtained from a twisted compactification of the 6D
A_{1}

(2,0)
theory:

\displaystyle\left(\begin{aligned} &\text{6D $A_{1}$ $(2,0)$ theory on $\mathbb{R}^{1,2}\times S^{3}$}\\
&\text{with a BPS codimension-two defect along $\mathbb{R}^{1,2}\times\mathcal{K}$}\end{aligned}\right)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\text{size}(S^{3})\to 0\hskip 8.19447pt}T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]\;.

(1)

A partial topological twist is performed along
S^{3}
using an
SO(3)
subgroup of the
SO(5)
R-symmetry of the 6D theory.
This twist preserves four supercharges, and the resulting 3D theory is expected to possess
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetry. The theory has a distinguished
U(1)_{R}
symmetry, often referred to as the
geometrical
R-symmetry, whose charge
R_{\rm geo}
can be identified as

\displaystyle\begin{split}R_{\rm geo}=\;&\text{the charge of }SO(2)\subset SO(2)\times SO(3)\\
&\hskip 56.00014pt\subset SO(5)\text{ R-symmetry of the 6D theory}\;.\end{split}

(2)

A characteristic property of this
geometric
U(1)_{R}
symmetry is that its charge is quantized,

\displaystyle R_{\rm geo}\in\mathbb{Z},

(3)

since it arises from a
U(1)
subgroup of
SO(5)
.
In addition, the theory enjoys an
SU(2)
flavor symmetry, denoted by
SU(2)_{m}
, which originates from the codimension-two defect:

\displaystyle\exists\;SU(2)_{m}\text{ symmetry in }T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]\;.

(4)

Brief Sketch of the DGG construction

The construction of
T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]
can be summarized as follows

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\textrm{an ideal triangulation $\mathcal{T}$ of $S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}$})+(\textrm{a choice of `polarizations'})\\
&\rightarrow\textrm{Neunmann-Zagier matrices }(A,B)\textrm{ and }\ g=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}\in Sp(2r,\mathbb{Z})\\
&\rightarrow T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]=\left(g\cdot[(T_{\Delta})^{\otimes r}]\textrm{ with superpotential }{\cal W}=\sum_{E_{I}:\textrm{easy internal edge}}{\cal O}_{E_{I}}\right)\;.\end{split}

(5)

In Section
2.1
, we review how to obtain the Neumann–Zagier matrices (NZ matrices) from an ideal triangulation of a knot complement together with a choice of polarizations on the triangulation. Here
T_{\Delta}
denotes a free chiral theory, and

\displaystyle g\cdot[(T_{\Delta})^{\otimes r}]

(6)

denotes the theory obtained by acting with an element
g\in Sp(2r,\mathbb{Z})
action on the free theory of
r
chirals
[(T_{\Delta})^{\otimes r}]
, using its
U(1)^{r}
flavor symmetry. For
r=1
, the
Sp(2,\mathbb{Z})=SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
action is the supersymmetric version of the Witten’s
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
action
Witten:2003ya
, and it extends naturally to general
r\geq 1
. The theory
g\cdot[(T_{\Delta})^{\otimes r}]
flows to an
{\cal N}=2
SCFT which contains 1/2 BPS chiral primaries
{\cal O}_{E_{I}}
associated with ‘easy internal edges’
\{E_{I}\}
, which will be defined in (
23
) and (
38
). By turning on superpotential deformations using these
chiral primaries, one obtains the theory
T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]
. A beautiful aspect of this construction is that the IR physics of

T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]

does not depend on the auxiliary choices—such as the ideal triangulation, the choice of polarizations, or the completion of the NZ matrices into an element of
Sp(2r,\mathbb{Z})
.
Different choices lead to different UV descriptions, but all of them are related by IR dualities.
In particular, different choices of polarization yield UV field theories that are related to one another by a sequence of the following basic
{\cal N}=2
duality
Aharony:1997bx
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{Basic ${\cal N}=2$ duality :}\;\;&\text{(a free theory of a single chiral multiplet)}\\
&\simeq\;\left(U(1)_{\frac{1}{2}}\;\text{coupled to a single chiral multiplet of charge $+1$}\right)\;.\end{split}

(7)

The most nontrivial step in the construction is to obtain a field-theoretic description of
g\cdot[(T_{\Delta})^{\otimes r}]
. In principle, one can decompose the element
g\in Sp(2r,\mathbb{Z})
into a product of the basic generators of
Sp(2r,\mathbb{Z})
-the called "S-type", "T-type" and "GL-type" transformations defined in
Dimofte:2011ju
. However, such decompositions quickly become complicated as
r
increases, and the resulting gauge theories are often far from simple. When
|\det B|=1
, the theory admits a much simpler description:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&g\cdot[(T_{\Delta})^{\otimes r}]\\
&=\bigg(U(1)^{r}_{K_{\rm eff}}\;{\cal N}=2\;\textrm{ CS theory coupled to $r$ chirals of charge matrix $\mathbf{Q}=\mathbb{I}$}\\
&\qquad\;\textrm{with UV effective mixed CS level }K_{\rm eff}=B^{-1}A-\mathbb{I}/2\bigg)\;.\end{split}

(8)

When
|\det B|>1
, the gauge-theory description above is no longer well-defined, since the matrix
K:=B^{-1}A
is not integer-valued and therefore the mixed Chern–Simons level fails to be properly quantized.
2
2

2

Here
K
denotes the bare mixed Chern-Simons level in the ”
(U(1)_{-\frac{1}{2}}
quantization”, whose entries are all integers.
The matrix

K_{\rm eff}:=K-\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbf{Q}^{T}\mathbf{Q}
denotes the UV effective Chern-Simons levels.

In Section
2.2
, we will address how to make the simple gauge-theory description sensible in this case.

A 3D–3D relation

One useful guideline for identifying the correct low-energy field-theory description is provided by the following 3D–3D relation
Terashima:2011qi
;
Dimofte:2011ju
;
Dimofte:2014zga
;
Cordova:2013cea
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Z^{T[M]}_{S^{3}_{b}}[X_{m}]:=\left(\textrm{BPS partition function of $T[M]$ on a squashed 3-sphere $S^{3}_{b}$ \cite[cite]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{Hama:2011ea}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}}\right)\\
&=\\
&Z^{SL(2,\mathbb{C})_{k=1}}_{M=S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}}[X_{m}]:=\left(SL(2,\mathbb{C})_{k=1,\sigma=\frac{1-b^{2}}{1+b^{2}}}\textrm{ CS theory partition function on }M\right)\;.\end{split}

(9)

The
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
Chern–Simons partition function on a knot complement

M=S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}
depends on a complex parameter
X_{m}
, which may be interpreted as the boundary meridian holonomy variable.
In the 3D–3D correspondence, this variable is mapped to the holomorphic mass parameter for a
U(1)
flavor symmetry of the theory
T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}]
. As we will see in Section (
2.2
), the theory
T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}]
always possesses a
U(1)
symmetry, which we denote by
U(1)_{m}
.
This symmetry can be identified with the Cartan subgroup of the
SU(2)_{m}
flavor symmetry introduced in (
4
).
More explicitly, the holomorphic parameter
X_{m}
is given by (
\hbar:=2\pi ib^{2}
)

\displaystyle X_{m}=\zeta_{m}+\Bigl(i\pi+\frac{\hbar}{2}\Bigr)\mu_{m}\;,

(10)

where
\zeta_{m}
is the rescaled real mass parameter,
\zeta_{m}=b\times(\text{real mass})
, for the
U(1)_{m}
symmetry, and
\mu_{m}
parametrizes the mixing of the
U(1)_{m}
symmetry with the R-symmetry. The R-charge at mixing parameter
\mu_{m}
is then given by

\displaystyle R_{\mu_{m}}=R_{\rm geo}+\mu_{m}T_{m}\;,

(11)

where

\displaystyle T_{m}\;\text{is the Cartan generator of the $U(1)_{m}\subset SU(2)_{m}$ symmetry.}

(12)

Here
R_{\rm geo}
denotes the distinguished geometric
U(1)

R
-charge
defined in (
2
), which originates from the
SO(5)

R
-symmetry
of the parent six-dimensional theory.

Accidental and IR-decoupled symmetries in
T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}]

The
U(1)_{R}
symmetry and the
SU(2)_{m}
flavor symmetry of

T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]
are the symmetries expected from the
six-dimensional compactification picture.
In general, however,
T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]
may possess additional
symmetries beyond these, as emphasized in
Gang:2018wek
.
From the six-dimensional viewpoint, such symmetries can be interpreted as
emergent accidental symmetries arising in the infrared.

In the bottom-up construction of the theory
T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]
in (
5
),
these accidental symmetries arise from the fact that the ideal
triangulation may not contain a sufficient number of
easy edges
,
defined in (
23
).
Each easy edge gives rise to a superpotential term that breaks one Cartan
generator of the flavor symmetry.
Consequently, when there are too few easy edges, some flavor symmetries
remain unbroken, leading to additional flavor symmetries beyond
SU(2)_{m}
.
The rank of the ultraviolet flavor symmetry of
T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}]

is given in (
46
).

This, however, is not the end of the story for flavor symmetry counting.
Some of the ultraviolet flavor symmetries of
T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}]

may decouple in the infrared, acting trivially on all IR observables.
To identify the faithful infrared symmetry, one must therefore mod out
such decoupled flavor symmetries, as in (
53
).
Both the accidental symmetries and the IR-decoupled symmetries will play
crucial roles in identifying the correct infrared phases of

T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}]
for the torus knot case,
i.e.
{\cal K}={\cal K}_{(P,Q)}
, as we will see in
Section
2.3
.

2.1
Symplectic structure in ideal triangulation

Gluing equations

We consider an ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
of a knot complement
thurston2022geometry
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\;:\;S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}=\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}\Delta_{i}\right)/\sim\;.

(13)

For each ideal tetrahedron
\Delta_{i}
, we assign the boundary phase space
P(\partial\Delta_{i})

Dimofte:2011gm

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P(\partial\Delta_{i})=\{Z_{i},Z_{i}^{\prime},Z_{i}^{\prime\prime}\;:\;Z_{i}+Z_{i}^{\prime}+Z_{i}^{\prime\prime}=i\pi\},\\
&\text{with symplectic structure }\;\;\{Z_{i},Z_{i}^{\prime\prime}\}_{\rm P.B.}=\{Z_{i}^{\prime},Z_{i}\}_{\rm P.B.}=\{Z_{i}^{\prime\prime},Z_{i}^{\prime}\}_{\rm P.B.}=1\;.\end{split}

(14)

The choice of
polarization
specifies the position variable
X_{i}
and the momentum variable
P_{i}

for each tetrahedron. For the polarization choice, one may take

\displaystyle\text{polarization choices :}\;\;\begin{pmatrix}X_{i}\\[2.0pt]
P_{i}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}Z_{i}\\
Z_{i}^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix},\;\;\text{or}\;\;\begin{pmatrix}Z^{\prime}_{i}\\
Z_{i}\end{pmatrix},\;\;\text{or}\;\;\begin{pmatrix}Z^{\prime\prime}_{i}\\
Z^{\prime}_{i}\end{pmatrix}.

(15)

The gluing equations consist of the internal-edge equations and the boundary equation:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\textrm{internal edges : }C_{I}&=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left(G_{Ii}Z_{i}+G^{\prime}_{Ii}Z^{\prime}_{i}+G^{\prime\prime}_{Ii}Z^{\prime\prime}_{i}\right),\qquad I=1,\ldots,r,\\
\textrm{meridian : }M&=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left(\alpha_{i}Z_{i}+\beta_{i}Z^{\prime}_{i}+\gamma_{i}Z^{\prime\prime}_{i}\right).\end{split}

(16)

Among the
r
internal edges, only
(r-1)
are linearly independent due to the relation

\displaystyle\sum_{I=1}^{r}C_{I}=2\sum_{i=1}^{r}(Z_{i}+Z_{i}^{\prime}+Z_{i}^{\prime\prime})=2\pi ir\;.

(17)

These gluing equations are known to satisfy the following symplectic relations
neumann1985volumes
:

\displaystyle\{C_{I},C_{J}\}_{\rm P.B.}=0,\qquad\{C_{I},M\}_{\rm P.B.}=0,\qquad\forall\,I,J=1,\ldots,r\;.

(18)

Hard/easy internal edges and "refined" NZ matrices

We will generalize the notion of internal edges and we call

\displaystyle C=\sum_{i=1}^{r}(n_{i}Z_{i}+n^{\prime}_{i}Z_{i}^{\prime}+n^{\prime\prime}_{i}Z^{\prime\prime}_{i})\textrm{ with $n_{i},n^{\prime}_{i},n^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$}

(19)

an
internal edge
if

\displaystyle\begin{split}&C\big|_{\mathcal{G}[\mathcal{T}]}=2\pi i,\quad\{C,M\}_{\rm P.B.}=\{C,C_{I}\}_{\rm P.B.}=0\quad(I=1,\ldots,r)\;.\end{split}

(20)

Here the gluing affine space
\mathcal{G}[\mathcal{T}]
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}[\mathcal{T}]=\Big\{(Z_{i},Z_{i}^{\prime},Z_{i}^{\prime\prime})_{i=1}^{r}\;:\;(Z_{i}+Z^{\prime}_{i}+Z^{\prime\prime}_{i}=i\pi)\big|_{i=1}^{r},\;(C_{I}=2\pi i)\big|_{I=1}^{r}\Big\}\;.

(21)

Note that every internal edge
\{C_{I}\}_{I=1}^{r}
satisfies the conditions. More generally, the internal edge can be written as

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\sum_{I=1}^{r-1}N_{I}C_{I}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}L_{i}(Z_{i}+Z_{i}^{\prime}+Z_{i}^{\prime\prime})\;.

(22)

For later use, we also define

\displaystyle\textrm{internal edge $C$ is }\begin{cases}\textrm{easy},\quad\textrm{at most one of $(n_{i},n^{\prime}_{i},n^{\prime\prime}_{i})$ is nonzero, for each $i$.}\\
\textrm{hard},\quad\textrm{otherwise}\end{cases}

(23)

Let

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\{E_{I}\}_{I=1}^{\sharp_{E}}\;:\;\text{the maximal set of linearly independent easy internal edges},\\[3.00003pt]
&\{H_{I}\}_{I=1}^{\sharp_{H}:=r-1-\sharp_{E}}\;:\;\text{a linearly independent set of hard edges}\\
&\hskip 83.00015pt\text{that are linearly independent of the set }\{E_{I}\}\;.\end{split}

(24)

Fixing a choice of polarizations, the
refined
Neumann–Zagier matrices
(A,B;\bm{\nu}_{x})
are defined by the linear relation

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}M\\[4.0pt]
H_{1}-2\pi i\\
\vdots\\
H_{\sharp_{H}}-2\pi i\\
E_{1}-2\pi i\\
\vdots\\
E_{\sharp_{E}}-2\pi i\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{X}\\
\mathbf{P}\end{pmatrix}+i\pi\,\bm{\nu}_{x}\;.

(25)

We assume that the matrix
B
is invertible, which can always be achieved by choosing an appropriate set of polarizations
Dimofte:2012qj
.

Example :
{\cal K}={\cal K}_{(P,Q)=(2,7)}

The torus knot complement can be ideally triangulated with
r=4
tetrahedra with following gluing data, see (
301
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&C_{1}=2Z_{1}^{\prime\prime}+Z_{2}^{\prime}+Z_{3}^{\prime}+Z_{4}^{\prime\prime},\quad C_{2}=Z_{2}^{\prime\prime}+Z_{4}^{\prime},\\
&C_{3}=2Z_{1}+Z_{1}^{\prime}+2Z_{2}+2Z_{3}+2Z_{4},\\
&C_{4}=Z_{1}^{\prime}+Z_{2}^{\prime}+Z_{2}^{\prime\prime}+Z_{3}^{\prime}+2Z_{3}^{\prime\prime}+Z_{4}^{\prime}+Z_{4}^{\prime\prime},\\
&M=Z_{2}^{\prime}+Z_{3}^{\prime\prime}-Z_{4}\;.\end{split}

(26)

Then,

\displaystyle\{E_{I}\}=\{C_{2},C_{1}\},\quad\{H_{I}\}=\{C_{3}\}\;.

(27)

When we choose the polarizations as follows

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}=(Z_{1}^{\prime},Z_{2},Z_{3}^{\prime\prime},Z_{4})^{T}\textrm{ and }\mathbf{P}=(Z_{1},Z_{2}^{\prime\prime},Z_{3}^{\prime},Z_{4}^{\prime\prime})^{T}

(28)

the NZ matrices are

\displaystyle A=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&1&-1\\
1&2&-2&2\\
0&0&0&-1\\
-2&-1&0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad B=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0&0\\
2&0&-2&0\\
0&1&0&-1\\
-2&-1&1&1\end{pmatrix},\quad{\bm{\nu}}_{x}=(1,0,-1,1)^{T}

(29)

2.2
Field theoretic construction of
T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]

Using the Neumann–Zagier matrices
(A,B)
together with an additional
integer-valued matrix
\mathbf{Q}\in M_{r}(\mathbb{Z})
, we define the theory

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q}]:=\;&\left(U(1)^{r}_{K_{\rm eff}}\ \text{coupled to $r$ chiral multiplets }\Phi s\ \text{with charge matrix }\mathbf{Q}\right),\\[4.0pt]
\text{with}\quad K_{\rm eff}:=\;&K-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathbf{Q}^{T}\mathbf{Q}\;=\;\mathbf{Q}^{T}\!\left(B^{-1}A-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{I}\right)\!\mathbf{Q}\;.\end{split}

(30)

We define the charge matrix by

\displaystyle(\mathbf{Q})_{ij}=\text{charge of the $i$-th chiral under the $j$-th }U(1)\;.

(31)

The matrix
\mathbf{Q}
is chosen such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{i)}\;&K:=\mathbf{Q}^{T}B^{-1}A\,\mathbf{Q}\;\in\;M_{r}(\mathbb{Z}),\\[2.0pt]
\text{ii)}\;&N:=\left|\frac{\det\mathbf{Q}}{\sqrt{\det B}}\right|^{2}\quad\text{is a square-free natural number}.\\[2.0pt]
\end{split}

(32)

We assume that such a choice of
\mathbf{Q}
exists.
In all examples considered in this work, we are able to find a matrix
\mathbf{Q}
satisfying these conditions.

Let
\Gamma^{[1]}
denotes the 1-form symmetry of
\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q}]
, which is given by

\displaystyle\Gamma^{[1]}=\frac{\mathbb{Z}^{r}}{K\cdot\mathbb{Z}^{r}+\mathbf{Q}\cdot\mathbb{Z}^{r}}\;.

(33)

Here the numerator
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
represents the Wilson-line charge lattice of the
U(1)^{r}
gauge fields,
while
K\cdot\mathbb{Z}^{r}
and
\mathbf{Q}\cdot\mathbb{Z}^{r}
correspond to the charges screened by the mixed Chern–Simons couplings and the charged matter fields, respectively. The
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
is non-anomalous when

\displaystyle\begin{split}&q([v]):=\frac{N}{2}v^{T}K^{-1}v\;(\textrm{mod 1})\;\;\textrm{is well-defined for $[v]\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}\in\Gamma^{[1]}(K,\mathbf{Q})$}\;,\\
&\textrm{and $q([v])=0$ for a generator $[v]$ of $\mathbb{Z}_{N}$}\;.\end{split}

(34)

We assume that the theory
\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q}]
develops an anomalous

\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry in the infrared, whose generating line operator
has topological spin
\tfrac{p}{2N}
.
This
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
symmetry may descend from a UV 1-form symmetry
(i.e.
\mathbb{Z}_{N}\subset\Gamma^{[1]}
), or it may instead be an emergent
symmetry appearing only in the infrared. The motivation for this assumption is as follows.
As we will see below, the theory
\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q}]
shares the same
supersymmetric partition function on the squashed three-sphere
S^{3}_{b}

Hama:2011ea
as the theory
g\cdot(T_{\Delta})^{\otimes r}
appearing in
(
5
), up to an overall factor of
1/\sqrt{N}
:

\displaystyle{\cal Z}_{S^{3}_{b}}\!\left[\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q}]\right]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\,{\cal Z}_{S^{3}_{b}}\!\left[g\cdot(T_{\Delta})^{\otimes r}\right].

(35)

This relation is highly nontrivial, since the partition function depends
nontrivially on the squashing parameter as well as on multiple real mass
parameters. The factor
1/\sqrt{N}
can be interpreted as the contribution of a decoupled
topological field theory
\mathcal{A}^{N,p}
, which is the minimal TQFT
carrying the anomalous
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry
Hsin:2018vcg
.
3
3

3

When
N
is not square-free, say
N=N_{1}^{\,2}N_{2}
, the theory

\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q}]
is expected to possess a non-anomalous

\mathbb{Z}_{N_{1}}
1-form symmetry.
Upon gauging this symmetry, the resulting theory is infrared equivalent to

g\cdot(T_{\Delta})^{\otimes r}
up to a decoupled

\mathcal{A}^{N_{2},p_{2}}
sector.
In this case, the local operator spectrum of

\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q}]
generally differs from that of

g\cdot(T_{\Delta})^{\otimes r}
, since gauging a 1-form symmetry—unlike
tensoring with a TQFT—modifies the spectrum of local operators.
In particular, certain monopole operators present in

g\cdot(T_{\Delta})^{\otimes r}
are absent in

\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q}]
.
Consequently, one should not expect the existence of a chiral primary operator

\mathcal{O}_{E}
in
\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q}]
associated with the easy internal
edge in (
5
).
For this reason, we restrict our attention to square-free values of
N
.

The infrared 1-form symmetry can be analyzed systematically using
supersymmetric localization.
Upon removing the decoupled topological sector, we define the theory
T[A,B]
by

\displaystyle T[A,B]\otimes\mathcal{A}^{N,p}\;:=\;\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q}]\,.

(36)

The theory
T[A,B]
is expected to be infrared equivalent to

g\cdot(T_{\Delta})^{\otimes r}
, possibly up to decoupled invertible TQFTs,
since the two theories share the same partition function on the squashed
three-sphere.

Easy edges and superpotential deformations

Now we turn to a superpotential deformation of the
T[A,B]
theory. We first need to identify the spectrum of chiral primary operators in
T[A,B]
. Interestingly, to each
easy
internal edge
E_{I}
of the ideal triangulation, one can associate a corresponding chiral primary operator
\mathcal{O}_{E_{I}}
.
Let
\{E_{I}\}
denote the set of easy internal edges.
Each
E_{I}
can be written as

\displaystyle E_{I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\big(g_{Ii}X_{i}+g^{\prime}_{Ii}R_{i}+g^{\prime\prime}_{Ii}P_{i}\big)\;\;\textrm{ with }g_{Ii},g^{\prime}_{Ii},g^{\prime\prime}_{Ii}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\;,

(37)

where for each
i
, at most one of
(g_{Ii},g^{\prime}_{Ii},g^{\prime\prime}_{Ii})
is nonzero.
Here
R_{i}
is whichever one among
\{Z_{i},Z_{i}^{\prime},Z_{i}^{\prime\prime}\}
is not equal to
X_{i}
or
P_{i}
. In the first line, the index
J
runs only up to
J=r-1
, because only
r-1
of the
C_{I}
are independent due to the relation in (
17
).
For each easy internal edge
E_{I}
, there exists a corresponding gauge-invariant chiral primary operator
\mathcal{O}_{E_{I}}
in
T[A,B]
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{O}_{E_{I}}&=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}\phi_{i}^{\,g_{Ii}}\right)V_{\mathbf{m}(E_{I})},\\
\mathbf{m}(E_{I})&:=\mathbf{Q}^{-1}\cdot(g^{\prime}_{I1}-g^{\prime\prime}_{I1},\ldots,g^{\prime}_{Ir}-g^{\prime\prime}_{Ir})^{T}=\mathbf{Q}^{-1}\cdot(\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{I}-\mathbf{g}^{\prime\prime}_{I})\;.\end{split}

(38)

Here
V_{\mathbf{m}}
denotes the
\tfrac{1}{2}
-BPS bare monopole operator of monopole flux

\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{r})^{T}
.
Its gauge charge
q_{i}
under the
i
-th
U(1)
gauge symmetry is

\displaystyle\begin{split}&q_{i}(V_{\mathbf{m}})=\left(\mathbf{Q}^{T}\cdot(B^{-1}A-\mathbb{I}/2)\cdot\mathbf{Q}\cdot\mathbf{m}\right)_{i}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}(\mathbf{Q}^{T})_{ij}|(\mathbf{Q}\cdot\mathbf{m})_{j}|\;,\\
&\textrm{or equivalently,}\\
&\mathbf{q}(V_{\mathbf{m}})=\left(\mathbf{Q}^{T}\cdot(B^{-1}A-\mathbb{I}/2)\cdot\mathbf{Q}\cdot\mathbf{m}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{Q}^{T}\cdot|\mathbf{Q}\cdot\mathbf{m}|\end{split}

(39)

\phi_{i}
denotes the scalar in the
i
-th chiral multiplet. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}\left(\big(\prod_{i=1}^{r}\phi^{g_{Ii}}_{i}\big)\right)=\mathbf{Q}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{g}_{I}\;.

(40)

Using these expressions, one can check that
{\cal O}_{E_{I}}
is indeed gauge-invariant:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{q}({\cal O}_{E_{I}})&=\mathbf{Q}^{T}\mathbf{g}_{I}+\mathbf{Q}^{T}B^{-1}\!\left(A(\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{I}-\mathbf{g}^{\prime\prime}_{I})-\frac{1}{2}\,B\big(\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{I}-\mathbf{g}^{\prime\prime}_{I}+\lvert\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{I}-\mathbf{g}^{\prime\prime}_{I}\rvert\big)\right)\\
&=\mathbf{Q}^{T}B^{-1}\!\left(A(\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{I}-\mathbf{g}^{\prime\prime}_{I})+B(\mathbf{g}_{I}-\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{I})\right)\\
&=\mathbf{Q}^{T}B^{-1}\!\begin{pmatrix}\{E_{I},M\}_{\rm P.B.}\\
\{E_{I},H_{1}\}_{\rm P.B.}\\
\vdots\\
\{E_{I},E_{\sharp_{E}}\}_{\rm P.B.}\end{pmatrix}=\mathbf{0}\,.\end{split}

(41)

Furthermore, the dressed monopole operator
{\cal O}_{E_{I}}
has spin zero, since

\displaystyle g_{Ii}\,(\mathbf{Q}\cdot\mathbf{m}(E_{I}))_{i}=0\qquad\forall\,i=1,\ldots,r\,.

(42)

Using the 1/2 BPS monopole operators, we finally define

\displaystyle T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]:=\Big(T[A,B]\ \text{deformed by the superpotential}\ {\cal W}=\sum_{E_{I}:\text{easy internal edges}}\mathcal{O}_{E_{I}}\Big)\;.

(43)

Equivalently, the theory can be defined by the relation

\displaystyle\begin{split}&T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]\otimes\mathcal{A}^{N,p}=\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q},\{E_{I}\}]\,,\\
&\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q},\{E_{I}\}]:=\Big(\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q}]\ \text{deformed by the superpotential }\sum\mathcal{O}_{E_{I}}\Big)\;.\end{split}

(44)

The relations among the four theories are summarized in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\framebox{$\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q}]$}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\framebox{$T[A,B]$}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\framebox{$\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q},\{E_{I}\}]$}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\framebox{$T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]$}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 8.19447pt\text{removing the }\mathcal{A}^{N,p}\hskip 8.19447pt}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cal W}=\sum_{I}\mathcal{O}_{E_{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cal W}=\sum_{I}\mathcal{O}_{E_{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 8.19447pt\text{removing the }\mathcal{A}^{N,p}\hskip 8.19447pt}

We expect that the infrared behavior of the theory
T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]

does not depend on auxiliary choices, such as the choice of polarization
(
15
) or the choice of
\mathbf{Q}
in
(
32
). In particular, different choices of polarization lead to different UV
descriptions, which are related to one another by a sequence of elementary

\mathcal{N}=2
dualities in
(
7
):

\displaystyle(\text{different choices of polarization})\;\longleftrightarrow\;(\text{different duality frames related by \eqref{mirror duality}})\,.

(45)

Different choices of polarization affect the Neumann–Zagier matrices in
(
25
), and consequently modify the admissible charge matrix

\mathbf{Q}
as well as the Chern–Simons levels,
K=\mathbf{Q}^{T}B^{-1}A\mathbf{Q}
, of the UV gauge theory

Flavor symmetry of
T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}]

The rank of the flavor symmetry
F_{\rm UV}
of the UV gauge theory

T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{rank}\,(F_{\mathrm{UV}})&=r\;(\text{number of tetrahedra})-\sharp_{E}\;(\text{number of easy internal edges})\\
&=\sharp_{H}+1\;.\end{split}

(46)

Here
r
is the rank of the gauge group
U(1)^{r}
, which is equal to the number of
topological
U(1)_{\rm top}
symmetries in the UV description.
Each easy internal edge gives rise to a superpotential term involving a chiral
primary operator, which explicitly breaks one Cartan generator of the flavor
symmetry. We define the vector space

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}[\mathcal{T}]:=\Big\{\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}\;\big|\;\mathbf{m}(E_{I})\!\cdot\!\bm{v}=0,\quad\forall\ \text{easy internal edges }E_{I}\Big\}.

(47)

This space can be identified with the Cartan subalgebra of the UV flavor symmetry

F_{\rm UV}
,

\displaystyle\big\{\bm{v}\!\cdot\!\mathbf{T}\;\big|\;\bm{v}\in\mathfrak{F}[\mathcal{T}]\big\}=\big\{\text{Cartan subalgebra of the UV flavor symmetry of }T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]\big\}.

Here

\displaystyle\textrm{$\mathbf{T}=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{r})^{T}$ denotes the charges of the symmetry $U(1)^{r}_{\rm top}$ in the $T[A,B]$ theory}\;.

(48)

A convenient basis of
\mathfrak{F}[\mathcal{T}]
may be chosen as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{F}[\mathcal{T}]&=\mathrm{Span}\big\{\bm{v}_{m},\bm{v}_{1},\ldots,\bm{v}_{\sharp_{H}}\big\},\\[4.0pt]
\bm{v}_{m}&:=\mathbf{Q}^{T}B^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\mathbf{0}_{r-1}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\bm{v}_{1}:=\mathbf{Q}^{T}B^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\\
\mathbf{0}_{r-2}\end{pmatrix},\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\ldots,\\
\bm{v}_{\sharp_{H}}&:=\mathbf{Q}^{T}B^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{\sharp_{H}}\\
1\\
\mathbf{0}_{r-1-\sharp_{H}}\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

(49)

One can check that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\forall\;I=1,\ldots,\sharp_{E}\;,\\
&(\bm{v}_{m}\cdot\mathbf{T}\textrm{ of }{\cal O}_{E_{I}})=\bm{v}_{m}\cdot\mathbf{m}(E_{I})\\
&=(1,0,\ldots,0)\cdot(B^{-1})^{T}\cdot(\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{I}-\mathbf{g}^{\prime\prime}_{I})=\sum_{j=1}^{r}(B^{-1})_{j1}(-B)_{I+\sharp_{H}+1,j}=0\;,\\
&(\bm{v}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{T}\textrm{ of }{\cal O}_{E_{I}})|_{i=1}^{\sharp_{H}}=\bm{v}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{m}(E_{I})\\
&=(\mathbf{0}_{i},1,\mathbf{0}_{r-i-1})\cdot(B^{-1})^{T}\cdot(\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{I}-\mathbf{g}^{\prime\prime}_{I})=\sum_{j=1}^{r}(B^{-1})_{j,i+1}(-B)_{I+\sharp_{H}+1,j}=0\;.\end{split}

(50)

In the
T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]
theory, there is always a
U(1)_{m}
symmetry, which can be identified with the
U(1)_{m}
in (
12
), whose Cartan
T_{m}
is given by

\displaystyle T_{m}=\bm{v}_{m}\cdot\mathbf{T}\;\textrm{with }\bm{v}_{m}:=\mathbf{Q}^{T}\cdot B^{-1}\cdot(1,0,\ldots,0)^{T}\;.

(51)

The
U(1)_{m}
symmetry is in fact the Cartan subgroup of an
SU(2)_{m}
symmetry
associated with the regular codimension-two defect inserted along

\mathcal{K}\subset S^{3}
in (
1
).
In addition to this
U(1)_{m}
, there are
\sharp_{H}
additional flavor Cartans.
These extra flavor symmetries are not visible in the 6D compactification picture
(
1
).

Some of the UV flavor symmetries may decouple in the IR, acting trivially on all
IR observables. For torus knot cases, this decoupling always occurs.
To analyze the structure of the decoupled flavor symmetry, we define

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\mathfrak{F}_{\rm decouple}[{\cal T}]\subset\mathfrak{F}[{\cal T}])\\
&:=\bigl(\text{the Cartan subalgebra of $F_{\rm UV}$ that decouples in the IR
for }T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}]\bigr)\;.\end{split}

(52)

As we will demonstrate in the next subsection, this decoupled
symmetry can be analyzed via BPS partition functions.
Then the Cartan subalgebra of the faithful IR flavor symmetry
4
4

4

More precisely, this refers to the part of the IR flavor symmetry that descends
faithfully from
F_{\rm UV}
. There may also exist emergent IR flavor Cartans
that are difficult to detect from the UV gauge theory description.

can be identified as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\rm IR}[{\cal T}]:=\mathfrak{F}[{\cal T}]\big/\mathfrak{F}_{\rm decouple}[{\cal T}]\;.

(53)

Example :
(P,Q)=(2,7)

Using the NZ-matrices in (
29
) and choosing
\mathbf{Q}=\textrm{diag}\{2,1,1,1\}
, we obtain

\displaystyle K=\mathbf{Q}^{T}B^{-1}A\mathbf{Q}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}6&0&2&0\\
0&1&-1&1\\
2&-1&2&-1\\
0&1&-1&2\\
\end{array}\right)\;,\quad N=\bigg|\frac{(\det\mathbf{Q})^{2}}{\det B}\bigg|=2\;.

Using (
36
) and (
38
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q},\{E_{1},E_{2}\}]\\
&:=\bigg(U(1)^{4}_{K_{\rm eff}}\textrm{ ${\cal N}=2$ gauge theory coupled to 4 chiral multiplets $\Phi$s in $\mathbf{Q}$}\\
&\qquad\;\;\textrm{with superpotential ${\cal W}={\cal O}_{E_{1}}+{\cal O}_{E_{2}}=V_{\mathbf{m}=(0,-1,0,1)}+V_{\mathbf{m}=(1,1,-1,-1)}$}\bigg)\\
&\textrm{where }K_{\rm eff}=\mathbf{Q}^{T}(B^{-1}A-\mathbb{I}/2)\mathbf{Q}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}4&0&2&0\\
0&\frac{1}{2}&-1&1\\
2&-1&\frac{3}{2}&-1\\
0&1&-1&\frac{3}{2}\\
\end{array}\right)\;.\end{split}

(58)

The UV gauge theory has a flavor symmetry
F_{\rm UV}
, whose Cartan subalebra is given as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}[{\cal T}]=\bigg\{\bm{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4})^{T}:\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0&1\\
1&1&-1&-1\end{pmatrix}\cdot{\bm{v}}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix}\bigg\}\;.

(59)

The vector space is spanned by
\bm{v}_{m}
and
\bm{v}_{1}
in (
49
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bm{v}_{m}=\mathbf{Q}^{T}B^{-1}\cdot(1,0,0,0)^{T}=(0,-1,0,-1)^{T}\;,\\
\bm{v}_{1}=\mathbf{Q}^{T}B^{-1}\cdot(0,1,0,0)^{T}=(-1,0,-1,0)^{T}\;.\end{split}

(60)

The theory has anomalous
\mathbb{Z}_{N=2}
1-form symmetry

\displaystyle\Gamma^{[1]}=\frac{\mathbb{Z}^{4}}{K\cdot\mathbb{Z}^{4}+\mathbf{Q}\cdot\mathbb{Z}^{4}}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{2}\;.

(61)

By removing the decoupled
\mathcal{A}^{N=2,p}
from
\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q}]
, we obtain the
T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(2,7)}]

\displaystyle T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(2,7)}]\otimes{\cal A}^{N=2,p}\simeq\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q},\{E_{1},E_{2}\}]\;.

(62)

2.3
T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}]
for torus knots
{\cal K}={\cal K}_{(P,Q)}

For torus knots, we find that the ideal triangulation of

S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}
, provided by the computer software SnapPy
SnapPy
,
always exhibits the following curious properties.
5
5

5

We have checked this for
(P,Q)=

\left.(2,2r-1)\right|_{2\leq r\leq 7}
,
\left.(3,3r-2)\right|_{2\leq r\leq 5}
,
\left.(3,3r-1)\right|_{2\leq r\leq 4}
,
\left.(4,4r-3)\right|_{2\leq r\leq 4}
,
(4,7)
,
(4,11)
,
\left.(5,r)\right|_{6\leq r\leq 9}
, and
(7,9)
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\bullet\textrm{There exists a special easy internal edge, say $E_{I=1}$, which is the sum of two edge parameters }\\
&\quad\;\textrm{from two ideal tetrahedra, }\textrm{i.e. $E_{I=1}=Z_{i}^{*}+Z_{j}^{**}$ { with }$Z^{*},Z^{**}\in\{Z,Z^{\prime},Z^{\prime\prime}\}$}\\
&\bullet\textrm{$r$ (number of tetrahedra)-$\sharp_{E}$(number of easy internal edges)}\\
&\quad=\begin{cases}1,&|P-Q|=1,\\[3.00003pt]
2,&\left(Q\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ P)\right)\ \oplus\ \left(P\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ Q)\right)\\[3.00003pt]
3,&\left(Q\not\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ P)\right)\ \land\ \left(P\not\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ Q)\right),\end{cases}\end{split}

(63)

For the
(P,Q)=(2,7)
case in (
26
),
C_{2}=Z_{2}^{\prime\prime}+Z_{4}^{\prime}
is the special easy edge,
E_{I=1}=C_{2}
, and
r-\sharp_{E}=4-2=2
. Based on the general counting given in (
46
), we predict the rank of the UV flavor symmetry
F_{\rm UV}
for the theory
T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]
to be:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\textrm{rank of $F_{\rm UV}$ of $T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]$}\\
&=\begin{cases}1,&|P-Q|=1,\\[3.00003pt]
2,&\left(Q\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ P)\right)\ \oplus\ \left(P\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ Q)\right)\\[3.00003pt]
3,&\left(Q\not\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ P)\right)\;\land\;\left(P\not\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ Q)\right),\end{cases}\end{split}

(64)

More explicitly,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\textrm{Cartan subalgebra of $F_{\rm UV}$})\simeq\mathfrak{F}[{\cal T}]\\
&=\textrm{Span}\begin{cases}\{\bm{v}_{m}\},&|P-Q|=1\\[3.00003pt]
\{\bm{v}_{m},\bm{v}_{1}\},&\left(Q\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ P)\right)\ \oplus\ \left(P\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ Q)\right)\\[3.00003pt]
\{\bm{v}_{m},\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2}\},&\left(Q\not\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ P)\right)\;\land\;\left(P\not\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ Q)\right)\end{cases}\end{split}

(65)

The basis vectors are defined in (
49
) with (
25
).
However, it is not guaranteed that this counting works in the IR. Actually, one of the Cartan generators of the UV flavor symmetry decouples in the IR due to the special internal edge.
The easy internal edge involving only two edge parameters plays a special role in the field theory
T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}]
.
If we choose a polarization such that
X_{i}=Z_{i}^{*}
and
X_{j}=Z_{j}^{**}
, the superpotential operator
{\cal O}_{E_{1}}
becomes

\displaystyle{\cal W}=\Phi_{i}\Phi_{j}\;.

(66)

It is nothing but a mass term, and the two corresponding chiral multiplets can be integrated out.
This superpotential lifts both of the
U(1)
flavor symmetries that rotate
\Phi_{i}
and
\Phi_{j}
,
whereas a generic single superpotential term lifts only a single
U(1)
.
One may say that the
U(1)
symmetry preserved by the superpotential
(namely the
U(1)
that rotates the two chiral multiplets with opposite charges)
effectively decouples in the IR.
Remarkably, this decoupled symmetry always turns out to be the
U(1)_{m}
symmetry defined in (
51
).
6
6

6

This can be checked as follows.
Choose a polarization in which
B
is invertible.
For this to hold,
X_{i}
(
X_{j}
) must be different from
Z_{i}^{*}
(
Z_{j}^{**}
);
otherwise, the
i
-th (
j
-th) row of the
B
–matrix becomes
\mathbf{0}
.
In such a polarization, the operator
{\cal O}_{E}
corresponds to
V_{\mathbf{m}}
with

\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}:=\mathbf{Q}\cdot\mathbf{m}=(\widetilde{m}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{m}_{r})^{T}=(\mathbf{0},\widetilde{m}_{i}=\epsilon_{i},\mathbf{0},\widetilde{m}_{j}=\epsilon_{j},\mathbf{0})^{T}

with
\epsilon_{i,j}\in\{\pm 1\}
.
One also finds that

\widetilde{\bm{v}}_{m}=(\mathbf{Q}^{T})^{-1}\cdot\bm{v}_{m}\propto(\mathbf{0},-\epsilon_{i},\mathbf{0},\epsilon_{j},\mathbf{0})^{T}
.
Here,
\widetilde{\mathbf{m}}
and

\widetilde{\bm{v}}_{m}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}
may be viewed as the monopole charge of
{\cal O}_{E}

and the generator of
U(1)_{m}
, respectively,
computed in terms of the rescaled
U(1)^{r}
gauge fields

\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}=\mathbf{Q}\cdot\mathbf{A}

(which are not necessarily canonically normalized)
such that the charge matrix becomes
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}=\mathbb{I}
.
In the duality frame with
(X_{i},X_{j})=(Z_{i}^{*},Z_{j}^{**})
,
the generators
\epsilon_{i}\mathbb{T}_{i}
and
\epsilon_{j}\mathbb{T}_{j}

are precisely the two
U(1)
symmetries rotating
\Phi_{i}
and
\Phi_{j}
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\text{$U(1)_{m}$ in $T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}]$ always decouples in the IR for }{\cal K}={\cal K}_{(P,Q)},\\
&\Rightarrow\;\bm{v}_{m}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\rm decouple}[{\cal T}]\;.\end{split}

(67)

From the BPS partition function computation, we will see that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\rm decouple}[{\cal T}]=\operatorname{Span}\{\bm{v}_{m}\}\;,

(68)

i.e. there is no additional decoupled symmetry other than the
U(1)_{m}
.
It implies that

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{F}_{\rm IR}[{\cal T}]\textrm{ in \eqref{F[T]_IR}})=\textrm{Span}\begin{cases}\{\emptyset\},&|P-Q|=1\\[3.00003pt]
\{[\bm{v}_{1}]\},&\left(Q\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ P)\right)\ \oplus\ \left(P\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ Q)\right)\\[3.00003pt]
\{[\bm{v}_{1}],[\bm{v}_{2}]\},&\left(Q\not\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ P)\right)\;\land\;\left(P\not\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ Q)\right)\end{cases}

(69)

Reduced UV description
T^{(\rm r)}[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]

By integrating out the chiral multiplets
\Phi_{i}
and
\Phi_{j}
in the theory
T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]
,
one obtains a reduced UV description

T^{(\rm r)}[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]
, in which the decoupled

U(1)_{m}
sector has already been taken into account.
This procedure simplifies the UV gauge theory by removing redundant degrees
of freedom associated with the decoupled sector.

At the level of the gluing equations, integrating out the chiral multiplets

\Phi_{i}
and
\Phi_{j}
corresponds to the following specialization:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Z_{i}^{*},\,Z_{j}^{**}\;\longrightarrow\;\pi i\,,\\
&\text{(all other edge parameters of the $i$- and $j$-th tetrahedra)}\;\longrightarrow\;0\;.\end{split}

(70)

In the 3D–3D correspondence, the edge parameter
Z_{i}^{*}
is identified with
the complexified mass parameter
Dimofte:2011ju
,

Z_{i}^{*}=i\pi\,R(\Phi_{i})+m_{\Phi_{i}},

where
R(\Phi_{i})
denotes the
R
-charge and
m_{\Phi_{i}}
the real mass of the
chiral multiplet
\Phi_{i}

Dimofte:2011ju
.
Therefore, this specialization corresponds to setting

R(\Phi_{i})=R(\Phi_{j})=1
and turning off the real mass parameters.
This has the same effect as introducing superpotential mass terms for the two
chiral multiplets and integrating them out in the infrared. Geometrically, the imaginary part of
Z_{i}^{*}
corresponds to the dihedral angle
between the two faces meeting at the internal edge.
Assigning it the value
\pi
implies that the
i
-th ideal tetrahedron becomes
completely flat and therefore effectively disappears from the triangulation.

After this reduction, there remain
(r-2)
linearly independent internal edges
\{C^{(\rm r)}_{I}\}_{I=1}^{r-2}
, which are linear combinations of the edge parameters of the remaining
(r-2)
tetrahedra.
Interestingly, these reduced internal edges still satisfy

\displaystyle\{C^{(\rm r)}_{I},C^{(\rm r)}_{J}\}_{\rm P.B.}=0\;,\quad\forall I,J=1,\ldots,r-2\;,

(71)

i.e. the symplectic structure among internal edges is preserved after the integrating-out procedure.
Upon choosing a polarization
(\mathbf{X}_{\rm r},\mathbf{P}_{\rm r})
for the remaining
(r-2)
tetrahedra, the reduced NZ matrices

(A_{\rm r},B_{\rm r},\bm{\nu}_{x;\mathrm{red}})

are defined by

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}H^{(\rm r)}_{1}-2\pi i\\
\vdots\\
H^{(\rm r)}_{\sharp^{(\rm r)}_{H}}-2\pi i\\
E^{(\rm r)}_{1}-2\pi i\\
\vdots\\
E^{(\rm r)}_{\sharp^{(\rm r)}_{E}}-2\pi i\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}A_{\rm r}&B_{\rm r}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{X}_{\rm r}\\
\mathbf{P}_{\rm r}\end{pmatrix}+i\pi\,\bm{\nu}_{x;{\rm r}}\;.

(72)

Here
\{H_{I}^{(\rm r)}\}
and
\{E_{I}^{(\rm r)}\}
denote, respectively, the sets of hard and easy internal edges after the reduction.
Then the theory
T^{(\rm r)}[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]
can be constructed from
(A_{\rm r},B_{\rm r})
in exactly the same way that one constructs

T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}]
from the original NZ matrices
(A,B)
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathbb{T}}^{(\rm r)}[A_{\rm r},B_{\rm r};\mathbf{Q}_{\rm r},\{{\cal O}_{E_{I}^{(\rm r)}}\}]\\
&:=\;\bigg(U(1)^{r-2}_{K^{(\rm r)}_{\rm eff}}\ \text{coupled to $r$ chirals }\Phi_{i}\ \text{with charge matrix}\ \mathbf{Q}_{\rm r}\\
&\quad\quad\;\;\textrm{with the UV effective mixed CS level $K^{(\rm r)}_{\rm eff}:=K_{\rm r}-1/2\mathbf{Q}_{\rm r}^{T}\mathbf{Q}_{\rm r}:=\mathbf{Q}_{\rm r}^{T}(B^{-1}_{\rm r}A_{\rm r}-\mathbb{I}/2)\mathbf{Q}_{\rm r}$}\bigg)\\
&\quad\quad\;\;\textrm{+ superpotential }{\cal W}=\sum{\cal O}_{E_{I}^{\rm(r)}}\;.\\[1.99997pt]
&T^{(\rm r)}[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]\otimes{\cal A}^{N_{\rm r},p_{\rm r}}:=\mathbb{T}^{(\rm r)}[A_{\rm r},B_{\rm r};\mathbf{Q}_{\rm r},\{{\cal O}_{E_{I}^{(\rm r)}}\}]\;.\end{split}

(73)

The chiral primary operator
\mathcal{O}_{E_{I}^{\rm(r)}}
can be constructed in
the same way as
\mathcal{O}_{E_{I}}
in (
38
). The two theories,
T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]
and
T^{(\mathrm{r})}[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]
, are expected to be IR-equivalent, possibly up to a decoupled Abelian TQFT:
7
7

7

As we will see in concrete examples, this decoupled TQFT is trivial in many cases. For example, for
(P,Q)=(2,*)
, the theory
T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]
does not contain a decoupled Abelian TQFT factor, and hence the Abelian sector is trivial.

\displaystyle T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]\simeq T^{\rm(r)}[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]\otimes(\textrm{Abelian TQFT})\;.

(74)

The decoupled Abelian TQFT may originate from dynamical (possibly discrete)
Abelian gauge fields in
T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]
that carry
non-trivial Chern–Simons terms and under which only massive chiral multiplets
are charged.

Since
(r,\sharp_{E})
in (
63
) reduces to
(r-2,\sharp_{E}-1)
, we expect that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\sharp^{(\rm r)}_{H},\sharp^{(\rm r)}_{E})=\begin{cases}(0,r-2),&|P-Q|=1\\[3.00003pt]
(1,r-3),&\left(Q\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ P)\right)\ \oplus\ \left(P\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ Q)\right)\\[3.00003pt]
(2,r-4),&\left(Q\not\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ P)\right)\;\land\;\left(P\not\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ Q)\right)\end{cases}\\
&\textrm{and }(\mathrm{rank\;of}\,F_{\rm UV})=\sharp^{(\rm r)}_{H}\;.\end{split}

(75)

More explicitly, the Cartan generators are given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{(\rm r)}[{\cal T}]=\textrm{Span}\begin{cases}\{\emptyset\},&|P-Q|=1\\[3.00003pt]
\{\bm{v}^{(\rm r)}_{1}\},&\left(Q\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ P)\right)\ \oplus\ \left(P\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ Q)\right)\\[3.00003pt]
\{\bm{v}^{(\rm r)}_{1},\bm{v}^{(\rm r)}_{2}\},&\left(Q\not\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ P)\right)\;\land\;\left(P\not\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ Q)\right)\end{cases}

(76)

Here we define

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{(\rm r)}_{1}:=\mathbf{Q}_{\rm r}^{\,T}\cdot B_{\rm r}^{-1}\cdot(1,\mathbf{0}_{r-3})^{T},\;\;\bm{v}^{(\rm r)}_{2}:=\mathbf{Q}_{\rm r}^{\,T}\cdot B_{\rm r}^{-1}\cdot(0,1,\mathbf{0}_{r-4})^{T}.

(77)

Example :
(P,Q)=(2,5)

The torus knot complement can be ideally triangulated with
r=3
tetrahedra with following gluing data, see (
250
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&C_{1}=Z_{1}+Z_{3},\quad C_{2}=2Z^{\prime}_{1}+Z^{\prime\prime}_{1}+Z_{2}+2Z_{2}^{\prime\prime}+Z^{\prime}_{3}+2Z_{3}^{\prime\prime},\\
&C_{3}=Z_{1}+Z_{1}^{\prime\prime}+Z_{2}+2Z_{2}^{\prime}+Z_{3}+Z_{3}^{\prime},\quad M=-Z_{1}^{\prime\prime}-Z_{2}^{\prime}+Z_{3}^{\prime\prime}\;.\end{split}

(78)

Note that the
C_{1}
is the special easy internal edge in (
63
). By taking the reduction procedure (
70
) on the gluing equations, we have

\displaystyle H_{1}^{\mathrm{(r)}}:=C_{2}^{\mathrm{(r)}}=Z_{2}+2Z_{2}^{\prime\prime}\;.

(79)

There is no easy internal edge after the reduction.
When we choose the following polarizations

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{\rm r}=(Z_{2}^{\prime})\textrm{ and }\mathbf{P}_{\rm r}=(Z_{2})

(80)

the reduced NZ matrices in (
72
) are

\displaystyle A_{\mathrm{r}}=(-2),\quad B_{\mathrm{r}}=(-1),\quad\bm{\nu}_{x;\mathrm{r}}=(0)

(81)

In the case, we choose
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{r}}={\mathbb{I}}
. Then,

\displaystyle\begin{split}K_{\mathrm{r}}=B_{\rm r}^{-1}A_{\rm r}=(2),\quad N_{\mathrm{r}}=\left|\frac{(\det\mathbf{Q})^{2}}{\det B}\right|=1,\quad\{E_{I}^{\rm{(r)}}\}=\emptyset\;.\end{split}

(82)

Using the above data, one can construct

T^{(\rm r)}[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(2,5)}]=\mathbb{T}^{\rm(r)}[A_{\rm r},B_{\rm r};\mathbf{Q}_{\rm r},\{E^{\rm(r)}_{I}\}]

as defined in (
73
).
This theory is precisely the UV gauge-theory description of the minimal

\mathcal{N}=4
rank-
0
SCFT studied in
Gang:2018huc
.

Example :
(P,Q)=(2,7)

By taking the reduction procedure (
70
) on the gluing equations in (
26
), we have

\displaystyle H^{(\rm r)}_{1}:=C^{(\rm r)}_{3}=2Z_{1}+Z_{1}^{\prime}+2Z_{3},\quad E^{(\rm r)}_{1}=C^{(\rm r)}_{1}=2Z_{1}^{\prime\prime}+Z_{3}^{\prime}\;.

(83)

i) When we choose
\mathbf{X}_{\rm r}=(Z_{1}^{\prime},Z_{3}^{\prime})^{T}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\rm r}=(Z_{1},Z_{3})^{T}
, the reduced NZ matrices are

\displaystyle A_{\rm r}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-2&1\end{pmatrix},\quad B_{\rm r}=\begin{pmatrix}2&2\\
-2&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{\nu}_{x;{\rm r}}=(-2,0)^{T}\;.

(84)

In the case, we choose
\mathbf{Q}_{\rm r}=\textrm{diag}\{1,2\}
. Then,

\displaystyle K_{\rm r}=(\mathbf{Q}^{T}B^{-1}A\mathbf{Q})_{\rm r}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&-1\\
-1&2\\
\end{array}\right),\quad N_{r}=\left|\frac{(\det\mathbf{Q})^{2}}{\det B}\right|=1,\quad{\cal O}_{E_{1}^{\rm(r)}}=\phi_{2}V_{\mathbf{m}=(2,0)}\;.

Using the above information, one can construct
\mathbb{T}^{\rm(r)}[A_{r},B_{r};\mathbf{Q}_{r},\{E_{1}^{\rm(r)}\}]
in (
73
), which is identical to
T^{\rm(r)}[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(2,7)}]
in the case since
N_{r}=1
. The theory has a flavor symmetry whose Cartan subalgebra is

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{\rm(r)}[\mathcal{T}]=\{\bm{v}=(v_{1},v_{2})^{T}\;:\;v_{1}=0\}\;.

(87)

The one-dimensional space is spanned by a vector
\bm{v}^{\rm(r)}_{1}=\mathbf{Q}^{T}B_{r}^{-1}\cdot(1,0)^{T}=(0,1)^{T}
.

ii) When we choose
\mathbf{X}_{\rm r}=(Z_{1},Z_{3})^{T}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\rm r}=(Z_{1}^{\prime\prime},Z_{3}^{\prime\prime})^{T}
, the reduced NZ matrices are

\displaystyle A_{\rm r}=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},\quad B_{\rm r}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
2&-1\end{pmatrix},\quad\bm{\nu}_{x;{\rm r}}=(-1,-1)^{T}\;.

(88)

In the case, we choose
\mathbf{Q}_{\rm r}=\mathbb{I}
. Then,

\displaystyle K_{\rm r}=B^{-1}_{\rm r}A_{\rm r}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-1&-2\\
-2&-3\\
\end{array}\right),\quad N_{\rm r}=\left|\frac{(\det\mathbf{Q}_{\rm r})^{2}}{\det B_{\rm r}}\right|=1\;,\quad{\cal O}_{E_{1}^{\rm(r)}}=V_{\mathbf{m}=(-2,1)}\;.

Using the above information, one can construct
\mathbb{T}^{\rm(r)}[A_{r},B_{r};\mathbf{Q}_{r},\{E_{1}^{\rm(r)}\}]
in (
73
), which is equivalent to
T^{\rm(r)}[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(2,7)}]
since
N_{r}=1
.
This theory coincides with the UV gauge-theory description of the
{\cal T}_{r=2}
theory introduced in
Gang:2023rei
, which flows to a rank-
0
SCFT that serves as the bulk theory for
M(2,7)
, up to a parity transformation.
The theory has a flavor symmetry whose Cartan subalgebra is

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{\rm(r)}[\mathcal{T}]=\{\bm{v}=(v_{1},v_{2})^{T}\;:\;-2v_{1}+v_{2}=0\}\;.

(91)

The one-dimensional space is spanned by a vector
\bm{v}^{\rm(r)}_{1}=\mathbf{Q}^{T}B_{r}^{-1}\cdot(1,0)^{T}=(-1,-2)^{T}
.

3
BPS partition functions and IR phases of
T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]

3.1
Proposed infrared behaviors of
T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]

Motivated by the intriguing observation of H. Hikami and A. N. Kirillov
Hikami:2003tb
, together with recent developments in non-unitary bulk–boundary correspondences
Gang:2023rei
;
Gang:2023ggt
;
Baek:2024tuo
;
Gang:2024tlp
, we propose the following:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]\;\simeq\;T^{(\mathrm{r})}[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]\otimes(\text{Abelian TQFT})\\[3.00003pt]
&\xrightarrow{\;\mathrm{IR}\;}\begin{cases}\text{unitary TQFT},&|P-Q|=1,\\[4.49997pt]
\mathcal{N}=4\text{ rank-0 SCFT},&\left(Q\equiv\pm 1\pmod{P}\right)\oplus\left(P\equiv\pm 1\pmod{Q}\right),\\[4.49997pt]
\dfrac{\text{a product of two rank-0 SCFTs}}{\text{(1-form symmetry)}},&\left(Q\not\equiv\pm 1\pmod{P}\right)\land\left(P\not\equiv\pm 1\pmod{Q}\right).\end{cases}\\[6.99997pt]
&\text{With an appropriate boundary condition, the IR theory supports the rational VOA of}\\
&{\cal M}(P,Q)\text{ on the boundary. For }|P-Q|>1,\text{ the realization of the VOA further }\\
&\text{requires a suitable topological twisting.}\end{split}

(92)

There exist earlier proposals for bulk field theories corresponding to the Virasoro minimal models—namely, the
{\cal M}(2,*)
series in
Gang:2023rei
and the general
{\cal M}(P,Q)
case in
Gang:2024tlp
. We expect that the theory
T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]
is IR dual to these previous constructions. In particular, the IR behaviors proposed above follow from the analysis in
Gang:2024tlp
.

An advantage of our proposal is that we have access to many duality frames, and by choosing appropriate ones and computing half-indices, we can reproduce various rational VOA characters—including the Virasoro minimal model characters. These different duality frames have a geometric origin in the ideal–triangulated 3-manifolds, arising from choices of polarization. Moreover, these polarization choices are fully understood in our framework, enabling us to identify the appropriate duality frame for half-index computations and to compute the half-index directly from the geometric data of the ideal triangulation. We will examine the half-index computation in detail in Section
4
.

In contrast, within the field-theoretic descriptions of
Gang:2024tlp
, it was not feasible to extract rational VOA characters from half-index computations due to the complexity of their UV gauge theories. A more direct relation between our
T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]
and the field theories proposed in
Gang:2024tlp
will be discussed in Section
5
, using Dehn filling operations in the 3D–3D correspondence.

When
|P-Q|=1

The theory has a mass gap and the IR theory is described by a unitary TQFT, where the
U(1)_{m}
symmetry and supersymmetry decouple.

When
\left(Q\equiv\pm 1\pmod{P}\right)\oplus\left(P\equiv\pm 1\pmod{Q}\right)

Under the RG, the
U(1)_{R}\times U(1)_{A}
, where
\mathfrak{u}(1)_{A}
can be identified with
\mathfrak{F}_{\rm IR}[{\cal T}]
in (
69
) or
\mathfrak{F}^{(\rm r)}[{\cal T}]
in (
76
), is expected to be enhanced to
SO(4)_{R}
symmetry of IR
{\cal N}=4
rank-0 SCFT. The generator of
U(1)_{R}\times U(1)_{A}
is embedded into
SO(4)_{R}
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&R\simeq\bigl(J_{3}^{C}+J_{3}^{H}\text{ of the IR SCFT}\bigr)\;,\\
&T_{A}\simeq\bigl(J_{3}^{C}-J_{3}^{H}\text{ of the IR SCFT}\bigr)\,.\end{split}

(93)

Here
J_{3}^{C}
and
J_{3}^{H}
are the Cartan generators of

SU(2)^{C}\times SU(2)^{H}\simeq SO(4)_{R},

normalized such that
J_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}/2
. This is the standard supersymmetry-enhancement pattern that produces IR
{\cal N}=4
rank-0 SCFTs from UV
\mathcal{N}=2
gauge theories
Gang:2018huc
;
Gang:2021hrd
.

When
\left(Q\not\equiv\pm 1\pmod{P}\right)\land\left(P\not\equiv\pm 1\pmod{Q}\right)

Under the RG, the
U(1)_{R}\times U(1)_{A_{1}}\times U(1)_{A_{2}}
, where
\mathfrak{u}(1)_{A_{1}}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)_{A_{2}}
can be identified with
\mathfrak{F}_{\rm IR}[{\cal T}]
in (
69
) or
\mathfrak{F}^{(\rm r)}[{\cal T}]
in (
76
), is expected to be enhanced to
SO(4)^{(1)}_{R}\times SO(4)^{(2)}_{R}
symmetry of the product of two
{\cal N}=4
rank-0 SCFTs. The Cartan generators of
U(1)_{A_{1}}\times U(1)_{A_{2}}
are embedded into the
SO(4)^{(1)}_{R}\times SO(4)^{(2)}_{R}
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&R\simeq\bigl(J^{(1)C}_{3}+J^{(1)H}_{3}+J^{(2)C}_{3}+J^{(2)H}_{3}\text{ of the IR SCFT}\bigr)\;,\\
&T_{A}^{(1)}\simeq\left(J^{(1)C}_{3}-J^{(1)H}_{3}\textrm{ of the IR SCFT}\right)\;,\\
&T_{A}^{(2)}\simeq\left(J^{(2)C}_{3}-J^{(2)H}_{3}\textrm{ of the IR SCFT}\right)\;.\end{split}

(94)

Example:
(P,Q)=(5,7)

The knot complement can be ideally triangulated using five tetrahedra, and the
corresponding gluing equations are, see (
474
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}C_{1}&=2Z_{1}+Z_{1}^{\prime\prime}+Z_{3}+Z_{3}^{\prime}+Z_{4}^{\prime}+Z_{4}^{\prime\prime},\\
C_{2}&=Z_{3}^{\prime\prime}+Z_{4},\qquad C_{3}=2Z_{2}+Z_{3}^{\prime}+Z_{4}^{\prime}+Z_{5}+2Z_{5}^{\prime\prime},\\
C_{4}&=Z_{2}^{\prime}+Z_{3}+Z_{4}^{\prime\prime}+2Z_{5}^{\prime},\qquad C_{5}=2Z_{1}^{\prime}+Z_{1}^{\prime\prime}+Z_{2}^{\prime}+2Z_{2}^{\prime\prime}+Z_{3}^{\prime\prime}+Z_{4}+Z_{5},\\
M&=-Z_{1}+Z_{1}^{\prime}-Z_{2}+Z_{2}^{\prime\prime}+2Z_{3}^{\prime\prime}\;.\end{split}

(95)

Note that
C_{2}
corresponds to a special easy internal edge in (
63
).
After performing the reduction procedure (
70
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}H_{1}^{(r)}&=C_{1}^{(r)}=2Z_{1}+Z_{1}^{\prime\prime},\\
H_{2}^{(r)}&=C_{3}^{(r)}=2Z_{2}+Z_{5}+2Z_{5}^{\prime\prime},\\
E_{1}^{(r)}&=C_{4}^{(r)}=Z_{2}^{\prime}+2Z_{5}^{\prime}\;.\end{split}

(96)

Here
C_{1}^{\rm(r)}
denotes the internal edge
C_{1}
after the reduction. Choosing
\mathbf{X}_{\rm r}=(Z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{5}^{\prime\prime})
and

\mathbf{P}_{\rm r}=(Z_{1}^{\prime\prime},Z_{2}^{\prime\prime},Z_{5}^{\prime})
, the reduced Neumann–Zagier data are

\displaystyle A_{\rm r}=\begin{pmatrix}2&0&0\\
0&2&1\\
0&-1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad B_{\rm r}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&-1\\
0&-1&2\end{pmatrix},\qquad\bm{\nu}_{x,{\rm r}}=(-2,-1,-1)^{T}.

(97)

Since
|\det B_{\rm r}|=1
, one may choose
\mathbf{Q}_{\rm r}=\mathbb{I}
, and
thus
N_{\rm r}=1
.
The reduced Chern–Simons matrix
K_{\rm r}
and the corresponding

\tfrac{1}{2}
-BPS operator
\mathcal{O}_{E_{1}^{(r)}}
are

\displaystyle K_{\rm r}:=B_{\rm r}^{-1}A_{\rm r}=\begin{pmatrix}2&0&0\\
0&-3&-2\\
0&-2&-1\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mathcal{O}_{E_{1}^{(r)}}=V_{\mathbf{m}=(0,1,-2)}\;.

(98)

From the factorized structure of the Chern–Simons matrix, we find

\displaystyle T^{(r)}[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(5,7)}]\simeq T^{(r)}[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(2,5)}]\;\otimes\;T^{(r)}[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(2,7)}]\;.

(99)

Here the theory
T^{(r)}[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(2,5)}]
can be constructed
from the data in (
82
), while

T^{(r)}[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(2,7)}]
follows from
(
2.3
).
This result is compatible with the general claim in
(
92
).
The factorization can also be made manifest in a UV description of

T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(5,7)}]
by an appropriate choice of
polarizations.
We leave the detailed analysis to the interested reader.

3.2
Superconformal Index

The superconformal index (SCI)
Kim:2009wb
;
Imamura:2011su
;
Dimofte:2011py
is a basic tool for analyzing the infrared phases
of three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
gauge theories.
When the index is identically equal to
1
, this strongly suggests that the theory
develops a mass gap and flows to a unitary topological quantum field theory (TQFT)
in the infrared.
If instead the theory flows to a three-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=4
rank-
0

superconformal field theory (SCFT) with enhanced supersymmetry, the index becomes
trivial in certain limits, known as the A- and B-twisting limits.
This is because, in these limits, the SCI computes the Hilbert series of the
Coulomb or Higgs branch, which is trivial for rank-
0
SCFTs.
Moreover, supersymmetry enhancement may leave characteristic imprints in the SCI,
contributions of extra supersymmetry
current multiplets.

The index for
\mathbb{T}[A,B;\mathbf{Q}]
in (
30
), which coincides with
the index of
T[A,B]
in (
36
) since the decoupled TQFT does not affect
the local operator spectrum, is

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\cal I}_{\rm sci}^{T[A,B]}[\bm{x},\bm{\mu}]:=\textrm{Tr}_{{\cal H}_{\rm rad}(S^{2})}(-1)^{R_{\bm{\mu}}}q^{\frac{R_{\bm{\mu}}}{2}+j_{3}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}x_{i}^{T_{i}}\\
&=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\oint_{|u_{i}|=1}\frac{du_{i}}{2\pi iu_{i}}\prod_{i,j=1}^{r}u_{i}^{K_{ij}m_{j}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}(x_{i}(-q^{1/2})^{\mu_{i}})^{m_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}{\cal I}_{\Delta}\big(\sum_{j=1}^{r}Q_{ij}m_{j},\prod_{j=1}^{r}u_{j}^{Q_{ij}}\big)\;,\end{split}

(100)

where
K=\mathbf{Q}^{T}B^{-1}A\mathbf{Q}
as defined in (
32
).
\mathcal{H}_{\rm rad}(S^{2})
denotes the radially quantized Hilbert space,
whose elements can be identified with local operators of the IR SCFT. Here
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})^{T}
are the fugacity variables for the topological symmetry
U(1)^{r}
(
U(1)^{r}_{\rm top}
) of the theory.
\bm{\mu}=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{r})^{T}
parameterize the mixing of the R-symmetry with the topological symmetry
U(1)^{r}_{\rm top}

\displaystyle R_{\bm{\mu}}=R_{*}+\bm{\mu}\cdot\mathbf{T}\;,

(101)

where
\mathbf{T}=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{r})^{T}
denotes the charges of the symmetry
U(1)^{r}_{\rm top}
as in (
48
). In the superconformal index formula, the reference R-charge
R_{*}
is chosen as

\displaystyle R_{*}(V_{\mathbf{m}})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\left((\mathbf{Q}\cdot\mathbf{m})_{i}+|(\mathbf{Q}\cdot\mathbf{m})_{i}|\right)\;.

(102)

The tetrahedron index
{\cal I}_{\Delta}(m,u)
, defined in (
502
), computes the generalized superconformal index
of a free chiral multiplet with R-charge assignment
R_{*}(\Phi)=0
, in the presence of
background Chern–Simons couplings
k_{FF}=-\tfrac{1}{2}
and
k_{RF}=\tfrac{1}{2}
.
Here
U(1)_{F}
is the flavor symmetry under which
\Phi
has charge
+1
, and

(m,u)
denote the background monopole flux and fugacity for
U(1)_{F}
.
Accordingly,

\prod_{i=1}^{r}{\cal I}_{\Delta}\!\left(\sum_{j}Q_{ij}m_{j},\;\prod_{j}u_{j}^{Q_{ij}}\right)

computes the generalized superconformal index of
r
free chiral multiplets
with
U(1)^{r}
flavor symmetry and charge matrix
\mathbf{Q}
.
The induced background mixed Chern–Simons levels are

K_{F_{i}F_{j}}=-\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathbf{Q}^{T}\mathbf{Q})_{ij},\;\;K_{RF_{i}}=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{j}Q_{ji}.

The R-charge of a monopole operator
V_{\mathbf{m}}
is then

R_{*}(V_{\mathbf{m}})=\sum_{i}K_{RF_{i}}m_{i}+\sum_{i}\frac{1-R_{*}(\Phi_{i})}{2}\,\big|(\mathbf{Q}\cdot\mathbf{m})_{i}\big|\,,

which give the expression in (
102
).
Note that the reference
R
-charge is quantized as

\displaystyle R_{*}\in\mathbb{Z}\;.

(103)

The superconformal index of
T[S^{3}\backslash\mathcal{K}_{(P,Q)}]
is obtained from

{\cal I}^{T[A,B]}_{\rm sci}[\bm{x},\bm{\mu}]
by restricting the parameters

\{\bm{x},\bm{\mu}\}
to satisfy

\displaystyle\begin{split}&R_{\bm{\mu}}(\mathcal{O}_{E_{I}})=R_{*}(V_{\mathbf{m}(E_{I})})+\mathbf{m}(E_{I})\!\cdot\!\bm{\mu}=2\;,\\
&\text{and}\\
&\prod_{i}x_{i}^{\,(\mathbf{m}(E_{I}))_{i}}=1\;,\qquad\forall\ \text{easy internal edges }E_{I}\;.\end{split}

(104)

Affine space
\mathfrak{M}[{\cal T}]
for R-symmetry mixings

The constraints on
\bm{x}
can be solved by

\displaystyle\bm{x}=\bm{\eta}_{\bm{v}}:=(\eta^{v_{1}},\eta^{v_{2}},\ldots,\eta^{v_{r}})\;\textrm{ with }\bm{v}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})^{T}\in\mathfrak{F}[\mathcal{T}]

(105)

The
\mathfrak{F}[{\cal T}]
is defined in (
47
).
To solve the constraints of
\bm{\mu}
, we introduce the affine linear space
\mathfrak{M}[\mathcal{T}]
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}[\mathcal{T}]:=\Big\{\,\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}\;:\;R_{\bm{\mu}}(\mathcal{O}_{E_{I}})=2,\ \forall\ \text{easy internal edges }E_{I}\Big\}\;.

(106)

There is a special point
{\bm{\mu}}_{\rm geo}
in
\mathfrak{M}[\mathcal{T}]
, which is

\displaystyle{\bm{\mu}}_{\rm geo}:=\mathbf{Q}^{T}\cdot B^{-1}\cdot{\bm{\nu}}_{x}\in\mathfrak{M}[{\cal T}]\;.

(107)

One can check that
8
8

8

Because
C_{I}-2\pi i|_{\cal G}=0
,

\mathbf{P}|_{\cal G}=-(B^{-1}A)\cdot\mathbf{X}-\pi iB^{-1}\cdot{\bm{\nu}_{x}}+B^{-1}\cdot({\cal M},0,\cdots,0)^{T}|_{\cal G}

with an arbitrary number
{\cal M}
. Then (
37
) can be written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left({(\mathbf{g}_{I}-\mathbf{g}_{I}^{\prime})^{T}+(\mathbf{g}_{I}^{\prime}-\mathbf{g}_{I}^{\prime\prime})^{T}\cdot(B^{-1}A)}\right)\cdot\mathbf{X}+(\mathbf{g}_{I}^{\prime\prime}-\mathbf{g}_{I}^{\prime})^{T}\cdot B^{-1}\cdot({\cal M},0,\cdots,0)^{T}\\
&\left.+\pi i\left({\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\mathbf{g}_{I}^{\prime})_{i}+(\mathbf{g}_{I}^{\prime}-\mathbf{g}_{I}^{\prime\prime})^{T}\cdot B^{-1}\cdot{\bm{\nu}_{x}}}\right)\right|_{\cal G}=2\pi i\;.\end{split}

(108)

As the first two terms vanish, it proves the identity.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall E_{I},\quad R_{\bm{\mu}_{\rm geo}}({\cal O}_{E_{I}})&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{I}-\mathbf{g}^{\prime\prime}_{I}+|\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{I}-\mathbf{g}^{\prime\prime}_{I}|)_{i}+\bm{\nu}_{x}^{T}(B^{-1})^{T}\cdot(\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{I}-\mathbf{g}^{\prime\prime}_{I})\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{I})_{i}+\bm{\nu}_{x}^{T}(B^{-1})^{T}\cdot(\mathbf{g}^{\prime}_{I}-\mathbf{g}^{\prime\prime}_{I})=2\;.\end{split}

(109)

We will argue that

\displaystyle R_{\bm{\mu}_{\rm geo}}\;=\;\left(R_{\rm geo}\text{ defined in \eqref{R_geom}}\right),

(110)

using the 3D–3D relation (
9
),
as will be demonstrated in (
147
). Using the
\bm{\mu}_{\rm geo}
as a reference point, the affine space can be given as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}[{\cal T}]=\bm{\mu}_{\rm geo}+\mathfrak{F}[{\cal T}]\;.

(111)

Similarly, one defines

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{\rm IR}[\mathcal{T}]:=\mathfrak{M}[\mathcal{T}]\,/\,\mathfrak{F}_{\rm decouple}[\mathcal{T}]=\bm{\mu}_{\rm geo}+\mathfrak{F}_{\rm IR}[\mathcal{T}]\;,

(112)

which parametrizes all possible R-symmetry mixings with the faithful IR flavor symmetry. In practice, the decoupled IR symmetries can be identified using the superconformal index as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{d}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\rm decouple}[\mathcal{T}]\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;&\mathcal{I}_{\rm sci}^{T[A,B]}\big[\bm{\eta}_{\mathbf{d}},\,\bm{\mu}_{0}\big]\textrm{ is $\eta$-independent at a }\bm{\mu}_{0}\in\mathfrak{M}[\mathcal{T}]\;.\end{split}

(113)

Using the basis in (
49
), we consider the following superconformal index for
T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}]
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\cal I}^{T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}]}_{\rm sci}[u,\bm{\eta};\mu_{m},\bm{\nu}]:={\cal I}_{\rm sci}^{T[A,B]}[\bm{x},\bm{\mu}]\big|_{*}\quad\bigl(\bm{\eta}:=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\sharp_{H}})^{T},\bm{\nu}=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{\sharp_{H}})^{T}\bigr)\\
&\textrm{with the replacement }\big|_{*}:\bm{\mu}\rightarrow\bm{\mu}_{\rm geo}+\mu_{m}\bm{v}_{m}+\sum_{I=1}^{\sharp_{H}}\nu_{I}\bm{v}_{I},\quad x_{i}\rightarrow u^{(\bm{v}_{m})_{i}}\prod_{I=1}^{\sharp_{H}}\eta_{I}^{(\bm{v}_{I})_{i}}\;.\end{split}

(114)

Here
(u,\mu_{m})
is the (fugacity, R-symmetry mixing parameter) for
U(1)_{m}
symmetry while
(\bm{\eta},\bm{\nu})
are that for other flavor symmetries. 3D-3D relation for the superconformal index is
Dimofte:2011py
;
Lee:2013ida
;
Yagi:2013fda

\displaystyle{\cal I}^{T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}]}_{\rm sci}[u,\bm{\eta}=\mathbf{1};\mu_{m}=0,\bm{\nu}=\mathbf{0}]={\cal I}_{S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}}^{SL(2,\mathbb{C})_{k=0}}(m=0,u;q)\;.

(115)

Here
{\cal I}^{SL(2,\mathbb{C})_{k=0}}_{S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}}(m,u;q)
is the 3D-index in fugacity basis, defined in (
501
), for the knot complement.

Torus knot case

For
{\cal K}={\cal K}_{(P,Q)}
, the 3D index becomes trivial when
m=0
, see (
504
):

\displaystyle{\cal I}^{SL(2,\mathbb{C})_{k=0}}_{S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}}(m=0,u;q)=1\;.

(116)

Thus the index is independent of
u
, as expected, because the
U(1)_{m}
symmetry of
T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]
always decouples in the IR, as stated in (
67
). Indeed, for torus knots, one can check that the full superconformal index is completely independent of both
u
and
\mu_{m}
:

\displaystyle{\cal I}^{T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]}_{\rm sci}[u,\bm{\eta};\mu_{m},\bm{\nu}]={\cal I}^{T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]}_{\rm sci}[\bm{\eta};\bm{\nu}]\;.

(117)

Furthermore, from explicit computations we have confirmed that, the index depends nontrivially on all fugacities
\bm{\eta}
as well as on the mixing parameters
\bm{\mu}
.
This verifies the claim in (
68
).

Using the basis
\{\bm{v}_{I}\}
of
\mathfrak{F}[{\cal T}]
in (
69
), the index is defined as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\cal I}^{T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]}_{\rm sci}[\bm{\eta};\bm{\nu}]\\
&=\begin{cases}{\cal I}^{T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]}_{\rm sci},&|P-Q|=1,\\[3.00003pt]
{\cal I}^{T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]}_{\rm sci}[\eta;\nu],&\left(Q\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ P)\right)\ \oplus\ \left(P\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ Q)\right)\\[3.00003pt]
{\cal I}^{T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]}_{\rm sci}[\eta_{1},\eta_{2};\nu_{1},\nu_{2}],&\left(Q\not\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ P)\right)\;\land\;\left(P\not\equiv\pm 1\ (\mathrm{mod}\ Q)\right).\end{cases}\\
&:=\begin{cases}\textrm{Tr}(-1)^{R}q^{\frac{R}{2}+j_{3}}\;,\quad R:=R_{\rm geo}:=R_{\bm{\mu}_{\rm(geo)}}\\
\textrm{Tr}(-1)^{R(\nu)}q^{\frac{R(\nu)}{2}+j_{3}}\eta^{T},\;R(\nu):=R_{\rm geo}+\nu T,\;T:=\bm{v}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{T}\\
\textrm{Tr}(-1)^{R(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}q^{\frac{R(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}{2}+j_{3}}\eta_{1}^{T_{1}}\eta_{2}^{T_{2}},\;R(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}):=R_{\rm geo}+\sum_{I=1}^{2}\nu_{I}T_{I},\;T_{I}:=\bm{v}_{I}\cdot\mathbf{T}\end{cases}\end{split}

(118)

The superconformal index for the reduced UV field theory
T^{(\rm r)}[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]
in (
73
) is (note that
\sharp_{H}^{(\rm r)}=\sharp_{H}
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\cal I}^{T^{(\rm r)}[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}]}_{\rm sci}[\bm{\eta};\bm{\nu}]:={\cal I}_{\rm sci}^{T[A_{\rm r},B_{\rm r}]}[\bm{x},\bm{\mu}]\big|_{*}\quad\bigl(\bm{\eta}:=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\sharp_{H}})^{T},\bm{\nu}=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{\sharp_{H}})^{T}\bigr)\\
&\textrm{with the replacement }\big|_{*}:\bm{\mu}\rightarrow\bm{\mu}^{\rm(r)}_{\rm geo}+\sum_{I=1}^{\sharp_{H}}\nu_{I}\bm{v}^{(\rm r)}_{I},\quad x_{i}\rightarrow\prod_{I=1}^{\sharp_{H}}\eta_{I}^{(\bm{v}^{(\rm r)}_{I})_{i}}\;.\end{split}

(119)

Here
\bm{v}^{(\rm r)}_{I}
is defined in (
77
) and
\bm{\mu}^{\rm(r)}_{\rm geo}
is defined as (cf. (
107
))

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}^{\rm(r)}_{\rm geo}:=\mathbf{Q}_{r}^{T}\cdot B^{-1}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{x;{\rm r}}\;.

(120)

The two theories are related by integrating out two massive chiral multiplets and
are infrared equivalent up to an Abelian TQFT,
as summarized in (
74
).
Accordingly, we expect their superconformal indices to coincide:

\displaystyle{\cal I}^{T^{(\rm r)}[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]}_{\rm sci}\big[\bm{\eta}\,;\,\bm{\nu}\big]={\cal I}^{T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]}_{\rm sci}\big[\bm{\eta}\,;\,\bm{\nu}\big]\;.

(121)

When
|P-Q|=1
, the 3D index becomes just 1:

\displaystyle{\cal I}^{T[S^{3}\backslash{\cal K}_{(P,Q)}]}_{\rm sci}(q)=1\;,\quad\textrm{when $|P-Q|=1$}\;.

(122)

It is expected from (
115
) and (
504
). A trivial superconformal index provides strong evidence for a mass gap, fully consistent with our main claim in (
92
).

When

\left(Q\equiv\pm 1\;(\mathrm{mod}\;P)\right)\ \oplus\ \left(P\equiv\pm 1\;(\mathrm{mod}\;Q)\right),

we find that there always exist two special values of
\nu
, denoted
\nu_{a}
and
